pill is now a choking hazard. There’s so much   about ET’s case that we just don’t know. Before 
she was born, what was that happening in her nasal   bone that was seen on the ultrasound? Because 
that’s where the cancer would grow 23 years later.   What were those episodes of sudden blindness and 
feeling cold on the left side of her face when ET   was 6? And why did they reemerge a year before she 
was diagnosed? Because that’s the side where the   cancer would grow. All that time was wasted with 
antibiotics when ET didn’t have a sinus infection,   was unfortunate. In almost every other case of 
a 23 year old woman having headache and nasal   congestion, a sinus infection would probably be 
the right diagnosis, it’s just in ET’s case it   wasn’t. And when she did get the diagnosis, it was 
something that doctors had never seen, because it   had only been formally described 6 years earlier. 
And the genetic mutation that defines the cancer,   is called multiple different names in literature. 
And even the cancer itself has only recently been   abbreviated SDSC, you can see the preceding 
literature didn’t have a standard name for   SMARCB1-deficient sinonasal carcinoma. One thing 
that we do know, is that ET did pay attention to,   and emphasized, the left side of faces and these 
are all the subtle signs and images given to us.   There’s been some developments in SDSC since 
ET’s diagnosis. But mostly in describing it,   and categorizing it. We still don’t know how to 
reliably treat it. All but a handful of hospitals   around the world can accurately diagnose it. And 
given that the mutation itself tends to create an   extremely aggressive, fast growing cancer, it 
means that getting the diagnosis wrong in the   first place, is a major issue with SDSC. One 
of the biggest problems with this isn’t even   medical. It’s not science. It’s getting in front 
of the right person at the right time, because   surgery can cure it, but it needs to be done early 
enough. Right now, we can only best help this by   raising awareness. In ET’s case, that time was 
possibly spent, waiting to see if antibiotics   would work for what was thought to be a sinus 
infection. 3 ½ months after being diagnosed,   ET no longer has the ability to move, speak or 
even open her eyes. Her siblings and parents   talk and read to her by her hospital bedside as 
she sleeps. At 6:30am on a sunny Friday morning, April 24, 2020, ET's breathing started to slow 
down and something sacred and ineffable happened. For the last two days, her eyes had been closed 
and she lay motionless in her hospital bed. But   this morning, all of a sudden, Ella opened her 
eyes wide, so wide, and looked deeply into my   eyes and through them to something beyond. A 
few moments later, her dad and her sister and   brothers came into the room. We were all together. 
We hugged her, we kissed her, we loved her and we   told her she should go when she was ready. Ella 
took her last breath only 3 ½ months after being   diagnosed with SDSC. Hi I'm Cassie Toulouse. And 
I'm Jean-Baptiste Toulouse. We're the parents of   Ella Toulouse, the real patient ET. About nine 
months before Ella started having mild headaches   in the evening, she had a couple of incidents 
where she saw double. She didn't mention them   to anybody and thought nothing of them. They 
reminded her of when she was very little,   and she'd been told by all the doctors who had 
done so many tests that everything was fine. She   only thought to mention these episodes once she'd 
been diagnosed. We wonder whether that cancer   hadn't been with her all along. Since she was 
very, very little, managed by her immune system,   somehow, until it got overwhelmed when she was 
23. And then, three months later, it was over.   If you know something is wrong and your doctor 
keeps dismissing it, you need to keep pushing and   get the exams that you need to rule out something 
more serious. Nobody expects a young person to   have cancer. If you have any symptom, if you feel 
like maybe this is something out of the ordinary,   maybe this is not sinus infection, or 
antibiotics are not working, do insist to   look for something else. It's important. Time 
is of the essence. We created SMARCB1 Hope,   an association with two purposes after Ella died. 
The first purpose is to put researchers, oncology,   specialists, doctors, in touch, and help research 
to find therapies and new approaches to cure these   cancer that are extremely difficult to fight. 
And the second purpose is to assist patients,   wherever they are in the world, to call on us, 
and we'll try to orient them so that they can   get the best information, the best care, and 
maybe clinical trials for new treatments. If   you are someone you know has been diagnosed with 
a SMARCB1 deficient cancer, please get in touch.   Please help us build this community around these 
cancers. So we can help researchers and doctors   find better treatments. Thanks so much for 
watching. Take care of yourself. And be well.

oxygen. This typically happens because of a blockage. The heart is metabolically
active, so, it needs a lot of oxygen to produce energy to function. When a heart attack happens, its often because
a blood clot gets lodged into a blood vessel supplying blood to the heart. A medicine that can quickly start to work
on this blood clot is.. Aspirin, which is almost always given to patients
with a suspected heart attack. LF had cared for these patients in the past,
and knowing that this could be happening to him now, he immediately tells his coach to
give him 4 aspirin tablets to chew, so that the medicine can absorb quickly into his body
as paramedics arrive. But, it didnt seem like anything was wrong. LF’s heart rhythm was normal— no signs
of a heart attack. His blood pressure and his heart rate, were
fine. Because he was only 28 years old, and was
previously healthy, it really didnt seem like anything was wrong. LF was still standing. He was responsive and oriented. This means his brain was fine. Paramedics told him to sit down and take a
deep breath, and he should be ok. They convinced him that he probably didnt
need to go to the emergency room, as they leave the gym. But as the hour passed, LF still had pain
in his chest. At home now, he laid down on his bed because
he thought he could sleep this off. A tingling in his fingers and his right leg
became more and more intense and his mind was racing, unsure of what was really happening. In the bathroom, nauseous. LF goes to empty his stomach into the toilet. But then suddenly his right leg becomes numb. He collapses to the ground. 911 is called, again, and LF is brought to
the emergency room where we are now. At examination, the medical team instantly
notices that LF’s right leg is a different color than his left, and that it’s cold
to the touch. This could be stroke. Instead of a blood clot in his heart, it could
be a huge clot clogging his leg, starving it of oxygen, causing it to become a different
color, and to become cold. LF was sent in for a full body CT scan, and
when the images returned, the medical team couldn’t believe what they saw. When someone experienced lifts really heavy
weights for compound movements, like squat, bench press or deadlift, they often perform
the Valsalva Maneuver. That is, they breathe in and hold their breath,
through almost the entire movement. The reason this is done is because when the
lift is performed, it’s ideal to keep every single part of the body as tight as possible,
because anything loose can become a point of failure. You have to do this while you’re lifting
very heavy. And the weights I am talking about are ones
that are so heavy, that when you are under the bar, you have thoughts that linger the
back of your mind that you’re not going to make it. I’m not talking about something like squatting
bodyweight on the bar, but rather 3, 4 or 5 times body weight. The problem is, when the breath is held in
like that, during that stress, it causes blood pressure inside the body to skyrocket. You can see it happen in real time because
powerlifters will have blood vessels pop out of their neck. The body is a closed and self contained system,
but this sudden, massive increase in pressure, can test the flexibility of the blood vessels. The heart starts to beat very hard and very
fast against this pressure, causing blood flow to become chaotic and turbulent. It’s well documented that this setting increases
shear stress against the walls of the arteries. In the overwhelming majority of the time,
for people who are healthy with no prior health problems, the body can handle this. In fact, when you’re lifting this heavy,
if you don’t do this in some capacity, you can severely injure yourself. But in the setting of an accident, something
else can happen. An in LF, there was an accident. The CT scan images showed that LF was experiencing

and dissection referring to a cutting open of. LF’s aorta had ripped open from the inside
close to where it connected to his heart. But this rip was contained to the inner linings
of the aorta. This explains his sudden chest pain radiating
to his neck, but what’s happening to his right leg? Immediately when seeing this, the medical
team knew they had to send LF to a larger hospital that could take better care of him. An ambulance was called. On the ride over, LF called his mom. He gave her bank passwords, social media logins,
and advanced directives on what to do, because as someone who was medically trained, he knew
what was happening to him. He knew he might have waited too long that
morning to get medical care, and he knew— there’s a chance that he’s not going to
come back from this. The ambulance arrived to the larger hospital
as the last thing he could remember. Surgeons quickly took a look at LF. Blood vessels are made of different layers. The inside is called the intima. Typically, when blood flows through, it kind
of rolls along this inside layer, through the hole of the vessel, called the lumen. When a tear evolves from the intima, blood
flow still “rolls” along that inner layer, but, blood is a fluid. If it can flow into something, it will. LF’s tear started from where the aorta emerges
from the heart. Blood started seeping into the tear, pushing
apart the layers of the aorta, creating a False Lumen. Blood stuck in here, doesn’t travel through
the body, rather, it continues down the aorta, separating the layers, before eventually emerging
through the aorta again, further down, by creating another tear. Surgeons repair the area where his tear originated. This should fix, most of his problems. And.. it seemed like he was doing well. But very quickly, the medical team finds that
it wasnt enough. The legs make up a high percentage of muscle
in the body. But when we look at how blood is circulated
from the heart, it’s almost like nature is telling us its priorities. When blood is pumped, some of it goes right
back to the heart muscle. Then it goes towards the brain, the arms,
the organs, and only after all of that, does it keep traveling to the legs. But, because the legs are so muscular, it
means that they need a lot of blood to function. But fixing the origin of LF’s aortic dissection,
didn’t fix the blood flow blockage in his right leg. The muscle start to starve of oxygen, and
it cant survive anymore. Muscles are covered by a layer of tissue called
the fascia. As LF’s muscle wastes away inside, the leg
starts to swell. The increased pressure further starves it
of oxygen. The swelling chokes out the vessels supplying
blood to the leg and squashes the nerves. If left untreated, his right leg will need
to be amputated. They send him in for surgery again, to make
some incisions and relieve the pressure. But just doing this won’t fix why his right
leg wasnt getting enough blood in the first place. So what was happening? This brings us back to False Lumen. The blood flow through this evolving tear
is inconsistent, because, it’s not supposed to exist. Even though the origin of LF’s aortic tear
was repaired, for some reason, blood in the False Lumen had started to pool, causing that
blood to clot, becoming like a jelly. The swelling is so great that it collapsed
the True Lumen of the blood vessel, stopping blood flow to the leg. This is called Thrombosis. Thromb meaning lump, but referring to blood
clot, and -osis meaning a disorder of. A disorder because of a blood clot inside
the False Lumen. In order to quickly get blood flowing again
to his right leg, surgeons go below the blood clot, and use a tube to connect it with the
artery supplying blood to his left leg. This is a temporary fix while they decide
how to proceed. When surgeons cut into his fascia to prevent
pressure from building up in his right leg, damaged muscle waste quickly spilled out and
started to float around in his body. Surgeons had gotten in at just the right time
because parts of LF’s leg, had already died. The kidneys are kind of like a net that filter
the blood. But as LF’s damaged muscles started collecting
in his kidneys, it started destroying them, blocking flow, preventing him from making
any urine. As the days pass, the medical team has to
irrigate and drain LF’s abdomen with fluid to try to catch wastes that should be caught
by his kidneys. He’s intermittently placed on dialysis,
a machine that filters his blood for him. But very quickly, the tube that was connecting
his left leg’s blood supply to his right leg became infected. Hospital bacteria started growing, and spreading
in LF’s body causing his heart beat and heart rhythm to become erratic. This isn’t too abnormal when one receives
this kind of surgery, but LF had taken care of patients with exactly the same problem
as he is having now. His heart beat was so uncomfortable, and he
was so scared that if he were to fall asleep by accident, that he wouldn’t wake up. But why did the aortic dissection happen in
the first place? Well, lets look at where the tear happened,
at the Aortic Arch. Do you remember turbulent flow? Well, when fluid flows through a bend, the
sudden change in direction tends to increase the amount of stress in the system. This stress has been shown to cause changes
to the inner lining of blood vessels. A lot of methods used to classify Aortic Dissections
come from different locations in the Aortic Arch. LF was a previously healthy 28 year old man
with no known health problems, but he did have multiple risk factors. Heavy weightlifting features more than just
the Valsalva Maneuver. Aortic dissections have been described in
people who lifted just an 80 pound [36.3 kg] dumbbell. There might have been a positional component
to this— that he was bent over in an awkward position, or he kicked the dumbbell back to
lay down on the bench to do his set. But also, there’s the component of LF taking
excessive amounts of caffeine and energy drinks that would have compounded with this heavy
lifting. Lets start with caffeine. It’s a stimulant. But it does that stimulating by blocking inhibition. In regular doses, like 400 milligrams or less,
it means you feel a bit more awake and alert, and maybe more productive. But at excessive doses all the time, rather
than blocking inhibition, caffeine gets the body to release adrenaline, which is going
to raise blood pressure. When you drink caffeine, it takes usually
4-6 hours for its level in your body to cut in half. That is, it takes around 4-6 hours for your
body to breakdown the caffeine. If you take a lot of it, and it increases
your blood pressure for hours everyday, and remember, most people continuously consume
more caffeine throughout the day, then you could reasonably expect that you could be
chronically increasing the amount of stress on the Intima through the Aortic Arch. That’s a potential risk factor, but it might
not have been the only thing happening in LF. Lots of pre workout supplements, also feature
a lot of caffeine. Sometimes it’s really the only actual thing
they have. And sometimes people take multiple supplements,
that all have caffeine. It’s all going to add up to much more than
400 milligrams daily. So, their blood pressure is high from just
supplements. Add in energy drinks. Add in more caffeine throughout the day. Then add in really heavy lifting. Add in that LF went to lift heavy on a winter
morning, a season and a time when Aortic Dissections appear to be more likely to happen. All of these risks can add up. And blood vessels are muscles. That means the aorta has its own set of blood
vessels that supply its blood, independent of the blood that it’s delivering to the
rest of the body. This is called the vasa vasorum. It’s possible that LF’s aortic dissection
wasn’t just a consequence of turbulent blood flow causing heavy stress on the intima, but
that other ingredients in his energy drinks or supplements, had also affected the integrity
of his small blood vessels. We’re never going to know for sure in his
particular case, but this has been described in literature. We know that supplements contain multiple
ingredients in unknown concentrations because it’s a “proprietary blend.” And nothing in this space is required to be
standardized, so every batch might be different. But we know… many people simply consume
way too much caffeine, often without realizing that it’s coming from many different sources
throughout the day. Sometimes from bread restaurant lemonade. There’s other complex situations too, such
as if LF was taking medicines for prior health problems, including ones for mental health. Those can increase blood pressure. But he wasn’t taking those. And neither was he taking performance enhancing
substances. LF was medically trained. Being able to have a career, pay his bills and make a living
depended on him having and maintaining his healthcare licensure. Even just having performance enhancing substances
in your possession, can get your license yanked, because many of them are controlled substances. And the overwhelming majority of reasonable
people would rather be able to have a career, pay their rent and bills for the next several years than
be able to lift a heavier weight. Can performance enhancing substances increase
the risk of aortic dissection in this setting? It looks like they might be able to. In the intensive care unit, LF’s surgeon
decides on a treatment plan. They will get access to his aorta through
his back while he is laid on his side. They will make a cut along his aorta where
the blood clot is, and take it out. Then, they’ll remove the part of the inner
lining of his aorta that was separated out, and pin down the remaining portion, before
closing the aorta up to allow blood flow through to his right leg, again. Valsalva maneuver in very heavy lifting is
necessary to keep your everything in your body tight throughout the whole movement to
prevent injury. What happened to LF was more than just Valsalva,
it was additional multiple risk factors and most importantly, it was an accident. I lived a very similar arrangement for many
years. I didnt lift in the mornings but… this could
have been me. As days become weeks in the hospital, LF’s
condition appears to improve. Nothing needed to be amputated. At physical therapy months later, he was able
to first stand up, and then walk again, and, he was able to make a recovery. Hey everybody my name is Luis Flores and I'm
the real patient LF. Right now I can still go to the gym, I can still
work out. I can't lift more than 35 pounds. I drive with my left foot. And I can still do patient care. Life goes on and I gotta just adapt with what
I can and can't do. One thing that I would have done differently
that day when I initially presented with those symptoms is that I would've went to the hospital
right away. That way I wouldn't have sustained the deficits
that I’ve sustained right now. However God works in mysterious ways. I'm just glad I that I went back and called that second ambulance. If you're at home or you're doing something
and you're starting to feel something that's not right, please get checked out immediately. I would have never thought at the age of 28 I would go through such a medical emergency, to the point
where in an ambulance I had to call my mother and tell her what my wishes would be. There’s always a saying in the medical field
that one day will all become patients. And now that I have the perspective of both
being the patient and the provider, that gives me an edge to be more and more of a compassionate
provider. My goal now is to get my Doctor of Nurse Practitioner
and I’ve just been accepted into Rush's Doctorate Program and so that’s some thing
that I have been looking forward to. My program starts in the fall. Best of luck in your studies, Luis. And thank you so much for watching. Be sure to checkout CoPilot link in the description. Take care of yourself. And be well.

extrapolation from both these animal reports,   and the background knowledge that 
calcium chloride, specifically,   has a high propensity of calcinosis due

started to feel like his regular self once again,   unknown the extent of permanent 
damage that may have been caused   by this self-inflicted episode. So we
